annual salary by approximately $12,000 per year. Because the Plan limits pension benefits to 100% of annual salary, Mr. Condit&#146;s benefit would have been reduced accordingly.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"> <B>
<A NAME="753tx27"></A>
EMPLOYMENT CONTRACTS AND TERMINATION</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>OF EMPLOYMENT ARRANGEMENTS</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><I></I><B>Employment Agreement With Mr. Stonecipher.</B>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Effective August 1, 1997, the Company entered into an employment agreement (the
&#147;Employment Agreement&#148;) with Mr. Stonecipher to secure his services as President and Chief Operating Officer of the Company. The Employment Agreement amends and restates Mr. Stonecipher&#146;s prior employment agreement with McDonnell
Douglas and supersedes all prior agreements between McDonnell Douglas and Mr. Stonecipher. While the &#147;Employment Period&#148; under the Employment Agreement was to expire on May 16, 2001, the Employment Agreement was amended effective June 26,
2000, to extend the expiration to May 16, 2002.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">During the Employment Period, Mr. Stonecipher is to receive both annual and long-term equity incentive compensation. Annual compensation includes a minimum base salary
of $900,000 per year, reviewed annually by the Compensation Committee of the Board of Directors. He received incentive compensation for 1997 of $635,000, paid 70% in cash and 30% in BSUs. Beginning with 1998, Mr. Stonecipher&#146;s incentive
compensation is determined under the Company&#146;s Incentive Compensation Plan. Pursuant to the Employment Agreement, the following long-term equity incentive compensation previously awarded to Mr. Stonecipher by McDonnell Douglas was converted
into similar awards with respect to Boeing stock in accordance with the terms of the Agreement and Plan of Merger between the Company and McDonnell Douglas Corporation: 477,415 Boeing stock equivalents (&#147;BSEs&#148;), 140,400 of which, together
with related dividend equivalents, remain subject to vesting by no later than March 31, 2002; 78,000 shares of restricted stock, 31,200 of which vested at the end of 1999 and 15,600 of which vested or will vest at the end of each of 2000, 2001, and
2002; and options to purchase 1,170,000 shares of Boeing stock, which vested and became exercisable in increments of 234,000 shares on September 24 in each of 1996, 1997, 1998, 1999, and 2000. Mr. Stonecipher receives dividends and voting rights on
his shares of restricted stock; the BSEs do not have voting rights, and dividend equivalent payments on the BSEs are reinvested into additional BSEs. All converted restricted stock and stock options will be issued under and subject to the terms and
conditions of the McDonnell Douglas Corporation 1994 Performance and Equity Incentive Plan. Additional long-term incentive awards will be granted to Mr. Stonecipher at the sole discretion of the Compensation Committee.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">For the purposes of calculating Mr. Stonecipher&#146;s benefits under the retirement plans of the Company and McDonnell Douglas, he will receive credit for twice as
many years of service as he actually worked for the Company and McDonnell Douglas. In addition, the Company will provide a supplemental pension payment equal to the difference between (a) what Mr. Stonecipher would have received from an employer
prior to his employment with McDonnell Douglas had he stayed with that employer through the end of the Employment Period and (b) the pension payments he is actually entitled to receive from the prior employer, McDonnell Douglas, and the
Company.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Pursuant to the Employment Agreement, Mr. Stonecipher is also entitled to at least four weeks paid vacation each year, fringe benefits and perquisites in accordance
with the policies of McDonnell Douglas as in effect immediately prior to the acquisition of McDonnell Douglas by Boeing, moving and relocation expenses incurred in moving to Seattle, and participation in the Company&#146;s other employee benefit
plans available to senior Boeing executives.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The Employment Agreement terminates upon the earliest of May 16, 2002, termination by the Company of Mr. Stonecipher&#146;s services for cause, termination of his
employment by Mr. Stonecipher for good reason, or Mr. Stonecipher&#146;s death or inability to render services for 180 days during any 12-month period. Under the Employment Agreement, &#147;good reason&#148; means a breach of the Employment
Agreement by the Company, removal from the Company&#146;s Board of Directors for reasons other than voluntary resignation, removal from his position as President and Chief Operating Officer for reasons other than for cause, diminution in
responsibilities or assignment of duties reasonably deemed by Mr. Stonecipher to be inappropriate for someone in his position.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">In the event Mr. Stonecipher terminates his employment for good reason, he will be entitled to receive the present value of the salary and target annual incentive
compensation he would have received if his employment had continued for the remainder of the Employment Period. In the event of such a termination, all of Mr. Stonecipher&#146;s BSEs would be paid upon termination of employment, stock options would
continue to vest for the remainder of the Employment Period and for one year following termination of the Employment Period and would be exercisable only within three years of that time, and grants of performance-based restricted shares would be
ratably adjusted. Payments of amounts due upon termination of the Employment Agreement will be deferred to the extent necessary to permit the Company a full deduction for all such payments under Section 162(m) of the Internal Revenue Code of 1986,
as amended.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The Employment Agreement prohibits Mr. Stonecipher from disclosing at any time confidential information or trade secrets concerning the Company without the
Company&#146;s express written consent. Mr. Stonecipher also may not be employed or affiliated with a competitor of the Company as long as any restricted stock, BSEs or stock options under the Employment Agreement remain unvested or unexercised. The
vesting of restricted stock and BSEs and the exercise of stock options are subject to Mr. Stonecipher&#146;s full compliance with the nondisclosure and non-compete provisions of the Employment Agreement.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"> <B>
<A NAME="753tx29"></A>
COMPENSATION COMMITTEE REPORT</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>ON EXECUTIVE COMPENSATION</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The Compensation Committee (the &#147;Committee&#148;) of the Board of Directors establishes and administers the Company&#146;s executive compensation programs. During
2000, the Committee was composed of five non-employee members of the Board&#151;Messrs. Biggs, Duberstein, Fery, and McDonnell and Ms. Ridgway. Dr. William J. Perry served on the Committee until his retirement from the Board on May 1,
2000.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The goals of the Company&#146;s integrated executive compensation programs are to</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">1.&nbsp;&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="90%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Attract, retain, and motivate a high caliber executive leadership team;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">2.&nbsp;&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="90%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Align executive compensation with shareholder interests;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">3.&nbsp;&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="90%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Link pay to Company and individual performance; and</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">4.&nbsp;&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="90%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Achieve a balance between incentives for short-term and long-term performance.</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Boeing executive officers are assigned to pay grades, each with an established salary range, a percentage of salary that establishes a target award for the annual
incentive, and a factor of salary on which long-term incentive awards are based. Assignment to a pay grade is determined by comparing individual responsibilities with industry survey data and internal executive job relationships. The survey data
compares Boeing pay levels with those for comparable jobs in a stable group of benchmark companies, which includes major aerospace and other large industrial corporations. The group includes two of the aerospace and defense companies in the S&amp;P
Aerospace Index used in the performance comparison graph on page 32.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Salaries</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The Committee annually reviews the competitive salary levels of executive officers. Boeing executive officer salary levels for the year 2000 were generally targeted for
the average of salaries of corresponding positions at the benchmark companies, as adjusted for company size.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Salary Determination.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</B>Executive officer salary adjustments are determined by a subjective evaluation of individual performance, and by
objective comparisons to internal peer data and external market data. Mr. Stonecipher&#146;s salary was determined in part in accordance with the terms of his employment agreement with the Company.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">After considering Boeing&#146;s overall performance, Mr. Condit&#146;s performance as CEO and competitive practices for CEOs at other major industrial corporations, the
Compensation Committee recommended, and the Board approved, an increase to his annual base salary effective March 1, 2000.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Annual Incentive Awards</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Annual incentive awards are designed to focus management attention on annual Company performance. Each executive pay grade has an assigned incentive target award. The
incentive target award percentages assigned to the Named Executive Officers&#146; pay grades range from 80% to 100% of salary. The target award is adjusted based on Company and individual performance. Executive bonuses are paid in a combination of
cash and Boeing Stock Units which are described in greater
detail below. For the Named Executive officers, including the CEO, the year 2000 awards were paid out approximately 60% in cash and 40% in Boeing Stock Units.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Incentive Award Determination.</B>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Each officer&#146;s target incentive award is adjusted first based on company performance against a
pre-established goal of economic profit, which reflects operating profit as well as cost of capital. The award is adjusted further based on an evaluation of individual performance. The Committee believes that the Company&#146;s financial performance
for the year 2000 exceeded expected levels.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Based on their contributions to the above-target performance, the Named Executive Officers received annual incentive awards averaging 191% of base salary. Because of
his leadership role and the Company&#146;s performance results, the Compensation Committee awarded the Chief Executive Officer an incentive award of 236% of base salary.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Boeing Stock Units.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</B>Forty percent of the annual incentive is awarded in Boeing Stock Units (&#147;BSUs&#148;), which serve to further tie
the interests of the executives to those of the shareholders and balance short- and longer-term performance focus. BSUs are restricted stock units without voting rights but earning dividend equivalents. BSUs vest three years from the date of the
award, assuming continued employment. The values of the BSUs at the time of grant to the Named Executive Officers, including the Chief Executive Officer, are shown in footnote (2) to the Summary Compensation Table on page 21.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Long-Term Incentives</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Performance Shares.</B>&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The Company&#146;s long-term incentive program is designed to link executive awards with shareholder returns. Under
this program, executives are awarded Performance Shares, which are rights to receive Boeing stock contingent on the Company&#146;s attaining shareholder return goals within a specified time period. These performance goals represent predetermined
annual five-year compounded growth rates from the stock price at the time the Performance Shares are granted. Any Performance Shares not achieving the stated performance hurdles within five years will expire. The terms of the Performance Shares
granted to the Named Executive Officers, including the Chief Executive Officer, are stated on page 23.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">During 2000, the Company&#146;s stock price achieved the stated performance hurdles required to vest 75% of the 1999 Performance Share grant, and 55% of the 2000
Performance Share grant. The dollar value of the shares awarded is shown in the &#147;LTIP Payouts&#148; column of the Summary Compensation Table.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Career Shares.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</B>The long-term incentive program also includes grants of Career Shares to certain executives who make substantial
contributions to the management, growth, and success of major components of the Company&#146;s business. Career Shares are stock units that are distributed in Boeing stock and contingent on the participant&#146;s staying with the Company until
retirement. Career Shares earn dividend equivalents, which accrue in the form of additional Career Shares and which will be distributed in Boeing stock when and to the extent the Career Shares are distributed. The number of Career Shares an
executive is granted is based on the executive&#146;s pay grade and salary. The values of the Career Shares at the time of grant to the Named Executive Officers, including the Chief Executive Officer, are shown in footnote (2) to the Summary
Compensation Table.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"> <B>Stock Ownership</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Stock ownership is a fundamental principle underlying the philosophy and structure of the Company&#146;s compensation programs. Stock ownership for executives ensures
alignment with the interests of shareholders and a focus on growing shareholder value. The Committee has established stock ownership guidelines for executives that range from one to six times annual base salary, and from four to six times base
salary for the Named Executive Officers.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Each element of the Company&#146;s incentive compensation program is designed to encourage and facilitate stock ownership, including payment of 40% of the annual
incentive award in BSUs, Performance Shares as the primary long-term incentive compensation program, and Career Shares that invest the executives in stock units until retirement. As a further incentive to encourage long-term stock ownership, the
Company provides a 25% matching contribution of any deferral of salary (up to 50%), annual incentive awards, and vested Performance Shares into an unfunded deferred stock unit account. The values of matching deferred stock units at the time of grant
to the Named Executive Officers, including the Chief Executive Officer, are shown in footnote (2) to the Summary Compensation Table.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Tax Deductibility of Compensation</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Section 162(m) of the Internal Revenue Code of 1986, as amended, imposes a $1 million limit on the amount that a publicly traded corporation may deduct for compensation
paid to a Named Executive Officer who is employed on the last day of the fiscal year. &#147;Performance-based compensation&#148; is excluded from this $1 million limitation.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">To the extent consistent with a performance-based approach and the Company&#146;s ability to provide competitive compensation, the Committee&#146;s policy is generally
to provide executive compensation that is fully deductible by the Company for income tax purposes. The Company&#146;s annual incentive awards and certain long-term incentive awards other than Performance Shares awarded to the Named Executive
Officers are designed to qualify as performance-based compensation that is fully deductible by the Company under Section 162(m).</FONT></DIV>

<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"> <B>
<A NAME="753tx33"></A>
PROPOSAL 2</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>SHAREHOLDER PROPOSAL</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>ON OFFSETS IN FOREIGN MILITARY CONTRACTS</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Several shareholders have advised the Company that they intend to present the following resolution at the Annual Meeting. In accordance with applicable proxy
regulations, the proposed resolution and supporting statement, for which the Board of Directors and the Company accept no responsibility, are set forth below. Approval of this proposal would require the affirmative vote of a majority of the
outstanding shares of Boeing stock present in person or by proxy and entitled to vote at the Annual Meeting.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Shareholder Resolution</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="4%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD ALIGN="left" WIDTH="96%" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">RESOLVED: Shareholders request the Company to disclose all significant promises (including technology transfers), made to
foreign governments or foreign firms in connection with foreign military sales, intended to offset their US dollar cost of weapons purchased by foreign nations.</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Proponents&#146; Supporting Statement</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">OFFSETS?</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Offsets are agreements by U.S. weapons manufacturers and the U.S. government to direct some benefits&#151;usually jobs or technology&#151;back to the purchasing country
as a condition of sale.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><I>Direct offsets</I> transfer purchasing dollars and/or work and military technology (often through licensing or joint production) to recipient countries to produce a
U.S. weapon system, or its components.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><I>Indirect offsets</I> may involve investments in the purchasing country, counter-trade agreements, or transfers of commercial technology.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">U.S. taxpayers finance offsets by (1) paying for the research and development of weapons and (2) providing grants, loans and loan guarantees for the sale. Offsets also
lead to U.S. job loss.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">PROPRIETARY?</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The U.S. arms industry guards offsets information, claiming &#147;proprietary privilege.&#148; However, purchasing countries often disclose such information, e.g., to
convince citizens of tangible benefits of millions of dollars spent on arms.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The proponents believe that insofar as U.S. arms manufacturers (1) engage in foreign policy by negotiating private offset agreements with foreign governments, and (2)
export jobs while claiming that foreign military sales create jobs, they forfeit their proprietary claims. Sound public policy demands transparency and public debate on these matters.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">EXAMPLES</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="2">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">In 1999, Boeing offered lucrative production-sharing contracts with Israeli military manufacturers, in connection with bidding on a contract with Israel. The <I>NY
Times</I> reported that Boeing promised
Israel $500 million in production-sharing business in Israel, and then raised the amount to $1 billion in local investments.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">1997 data shows that 13 U.S. prime military contractors reported 58 new offset agreements valued at $3.85 billion in support of $5.84 billion in export contracts.
Aerospace is the sector most impacted by offsets. Between 1993 and 1997, about 90% of offset agreements and transactions were associated with aerospace exports. In the case of offset transactions, however, not all the products were actually
themselves aerospace-related. Of the $11.8 billion in offset transactions, only 54% ($6.4 billion) were identified as aerospace products. (This may be understated because of difficulty in properly identifying a large number of products listed as
indirect offsets.) Between 1993 and 1997 U.S. defense companies entered into new offset agreements valued at $19 billion in support of $35 billion worth of defense contracts. For every dollar a U.S. company received from an arms sale associated with
offsets, it returned 54 cents worth of offset obligations to the purchasing country (&#147;Offsets in Defense Trade 1999,&#148; Commerce Department).</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">That Report concludes: &#147;offsets provide substantial benefits to foreign firms, and in the process deny business to otherwise competitive U.S.
firms.&#148;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">ARMS EXPORTS DON&#146;T CREATE JOBS</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The faith-based proponents submit this resolution for Board consideration because arms exports do not create jobs. Weapons proliferation and the export of jobs and
technology through offsets raise profound moral and ethical, as well as fiscal, questions that shareholders should address.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Board of Directors&#146; Response</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Boeing is a global company conducting business in the international marketplace. A portion of the Company&#146;s international business consists of the sale of military
equipment. These sales of military equipment and services require compliance with strict U.S. government regulations that control where products may be sold overseas and what products may be exported. The Department of Defense administers a rigorous
regime of approval for the sale of military equipment and permits only those sales that are consistent with U.S. interests and policy objectives. Government regulations also impose strict licensing controls on the export of equipment and technology
to protect the long-term economic and national security interests of the United States.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Contractual agreements for offset transactions, such as technology licensing, production sharing or co-marketing, are commonplace in international sales for both
commercial and military products. <B>To the extent the Company engages in such transactions, it does so only in compliance with U.S. government regulations and as negotiated with the customer. Failure to enter into such arrangements could result in
the loss of customers, and could translate into lost jobs and diminished shareholder value.</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The Company&#146;s offset arrangements provide the means for the Company to create new jobs and maintain its current labor force. The Company believes that robust and
bilateral trading leads to job creation at both ends of international transactions and is beneficial to international economic stability and growth.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The Company understands that some shareholders may wish to know details of the Company&#146;s military sales contracts with respect to offset transactions. However,
much of the information
requested is competitively sensitive. In addition, many offset arrangements are subject to confidentiality agreements with the customer that prohibit the Company from making such disclosures. U.S. law recognizes this fact and affords confidential
treatment to information that is reported to the government regarding offset transactions. Publication of such information would put Boeing at a disadvantage in its business, may breach contractual arrangements and would not be in the best interest
of the Company or its shareholders.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">U.S. government regulations require the Company to report to the Department of State extensive information regarding export of military products, including specific
information with respect to offset transactions. This government regulation is evidenced by the Defense Offsets Disclosure Act of 1999 (the &#147;1999 Act&#148;), which requires a description of any offset agreements with respect to foreign military
sales or direct commercial sales. Such reporting requirements ensure that the Company&#146;s offset transactions are conducted in strict compliance with U.S. government regulations.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The 1999 Act also establishes a National Commission on the Use of Offsets in Defense Trade. The National Commission, of which Mr. Condit is a member, is comprised of
representatives from government and the private sector, including industry (both defense and nondefense), labor and academia. The National Commission&#146;s charge is to review and prepare a report for appropriate congressional committees on all
aspects of the use of offsets in the international defense trade, including:</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE BORDER="0" WIDTH="100%" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="2%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"></FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="Wingdings">&#159;</FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000"
FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="98%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">current practices by foreign governments in requiring offsets in purchasing agreements,</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE BORDER="0" WIDTH="100%" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="2%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"></FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="Wingdings">&#159;</FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000"
FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="98%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">the extent and nature of offsets offered by U.S. and foreign defense contractors,</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE BORDER="0" WIDTH="100%" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="2%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"></FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="Wingdings">&#159;</FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000"
FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="98%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">the impact of the use of offsets on defense subcontractors and nondefense industrial sectors affected by indirect offsets,
and</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE BORDER="0" WIDTH="100%" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="2%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"></FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="Wingdings">&#159;</FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000"
FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="98%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">the role of offsets, both direct and indirect, on domestic industry stability, U.S. competitiveness, and national
security.</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The Company believes the work of the National Commission will address many of the proponents&#146; concerns, and is the proper forum for a review of and report on the
issue of offsets in foreign military sales.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>T<SMALL>HE </SMALL>

<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>A V<SMALL>OTE </SMALL>
AGAINST P<SMALL>ROPOSAL </SMALL>
2.</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"> <B>
<A NAME="753tx36"></A>
PROPOSAL 3</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>SHAREHOLDER PROPOSAL</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>ON MILITARY ACTIVITIES IN SPACE</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Several shareholders have advised the Company that they intend to present the following resolution at the Annual Meeting. In accordance with applicable proxy
regulations, the proposed resolution and supporting statement, for which the Board of Directors and the Company accept no responsibility, are set forth below. Approval of this proposal would require the affirmative vote of a majority of the
outstanding shares of Boeing stock present in person or by proxy and entitled to vote at the Annual Meeting.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Shareholder Resolution</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">WHEREAS: During the next 20 years, the United States military plans to develop technologies with the potential to wage war from
space;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Plans for this are explicitly laid out in documents such as <I>Vision for 2020</I>, a 1996 report of the US Space Command which
coordinates the use of the Army, Naval and Air Force Space Forces;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">This Report &#147;serves as a vector for the evolution of military space strategy into the 21st century.&#148; During the early portion
of the 21st century, it states, &#147;space power will&nbsp;.&nbsp;.&nbsp;.&nbsp;evolve into a separate and equal medium of warfare;&#148;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">A July 1999 Pentagon space policy paper directs the Space Command to start developing tactics and doctrine for conducting warfare in the
heavens;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The US Space Command believes that &#147;accelerating rates of technological development will be increasingly driven by the commercial
sector, not the military.&#148;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The development of the 1998 <I>Long Range Plan</I> of the US Space Command was assisted by 75 commercial entities, including Boeing
Company;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Some military analysts believe that US companies are likely to invest $500 billion in space by 2010, hence, the military will be called
upon to defend American interests in space;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Critics of the development of space weapons, including a member of the Senate Armed Forces Committee, believe that such a development
would be &#145;&#145;a mistake of historic proportions&#146;&#146; that would trigger an arms race in space;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">On Nov. 1, 1999, in the UN General Assembly resolution, &#145;&#145;Prevention of an Arms Race in Outer Space,&#148; 138 nations
reaffirmed <I>The Outer Space Treaty of 1967</I>, and specifically its provision that reserves space &#147;for peaceful purposes;&#148;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="5%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED:&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The shareholders request the Board of Directors to provide a comprehensive report
describing our Company&#146;s involvement in space-based weaponization. The report would be made available to shareholders on request within six months of the 2001 annual meeting. It may omit proprietary information and be prepared at reasonable
cost.</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"> <B>Proponents&#146; Supporting Statement</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The proponents of this resolution believe that outer space is the common heritage of all and should be used for peaceful purposes, for economic, scientific and cultural
development, for the well-being of all peoples. We also believe that shareholders deserve company transparency with regard to our company&#146;s involvement in research, development and promotion of weapons for space. Thus the request for this
report which could describe the following aspects:</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE BORDER="0" WIDTH="100%" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="2%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"></FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="Wingdings">&#159;</FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000"
FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="98%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Current value of outstanding contracts to develop components of the Space Command&#146;s programs;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE BORDER="0" WIDTH="100%" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="2%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"></FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="Wingdings">&#159;</FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000"
FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="98%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Amount of the company&#146;s own money (versus government funding) spent on in-house research and development in this segment
of its business;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE BORDER="0" WIDTH="100%" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="2%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"></FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="Wingdings">&#159;</FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000"
FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="98%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The ethical and financial reasons for being involved in the Space Command program.</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">We urge shareholder support for this resolution.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Board of Directors&#146; Response</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Boeing enjoys a rich heritage of space and communications progress and it has established a series of market aligned businesses at the leading edge of national and
international space and communications achievement. The space-based communications and services marketplace represents an exciting and burgeoning market in which Boeing is already and will continue to be a major participant. Space figures
prominently into the Company&#146;s push to develop new products and services. It is the Company&#146;s goal to become the world leader in integrated, space-based information and communications.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The U.S. government relies on the technological and manufacturing capability of the private sector to produce the equipment it has determined will be needed to achieve
a sound defense posture. Boeing, with its technological capabilities and expertise, participates in the nation&#146;s defense activities, including the use of weapons in space, in the belief that it is appropriate to support governmental decisions
made in our open, democratic society in the quest for peace and national security.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The Company&#146;s policy is to report to its shareholders on matters of significant interest. This reporting is a continuing responsibility and concern of the
Company&#146;s management. Information on its space-related operations is provided in the Company&#146;s annual reports, quarterly earnings announcements, and other statements to the press. The annual reports carry a description of the
Company&#146;s activities in some detail and are consistent with management&#146;s basic responsibility to discuss the business operations of the Company.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">We do not believe that the Company should be required to provide the special report requested by this resolution.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>T<SMALL>HE </SMALL>
B<SMALL>OARD OF </SMALL>
D<SMALL>IRECTORS </SMALL>

AGAINST P<SMALL>ROPOSAL </SMALL>

<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"> <B>
<A NAME="753tx38"></A>
PROPOSAL 4</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>SHAREHOLDER PROPOSAL ON LINKING</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>EXECUTIVE COMPENSATION TO SOCIAL PERFORMANCE</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Several shareholders have advised the Company that they intend to present the following resolution at the Annual Meeting. In accordance with applicable proxy
regulations, the proposed resolution and supporting statement, for which the Board of Directors and the Company accept no responsibility, are set forth below. Approval of this proposal would require the affirmative vote of a majority of the
outstanding shares of Boeing stock present in person or by proxy and entitled to vote at the Annual Meeting.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Shareholder Resolution and Supporting Statement</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">WHEREAS: Increases in executive compensation outpace employee wage increases. Base pay of CEOs accelerated 6.1% in 1998, while overall U.S. wages and benefits climbed
3.5%.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE BORDER="0" WIDTH="100%" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="2%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"></FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="Wingdings">&#159;</FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000"
FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="98%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">According to <I>The Wall Street Journal&#146;s</I> annual Executive Pay survey (04/06/00) &#147;salaries and bonuses of
surveyed CEOs jumped 11%&#148; in 1999, surpassing &#147;the 4.2% uptick in pay among white-collar employees.&#148; The same study showed that the salary and bonus for our CEO, Philip M. Condit, was $4,257,900. Meanwhile, total shareholder return of
Boeing stock for the past 5 years as compared to our industry was -10.1. <I>Business Week&#146;s</I> April 17, 2000, Executive Compensation Scoreboard listed Boeing next to the bottom among its aerospace peers in 1997-99 analysis of pay vs.
corporate profit.</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE BORDER="0" WIDTH="100%" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="2%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"></FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="Wingdings">&#159;</FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000"
FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="98%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">While the company&#146;s performance recently witnessed a turnaround, its previous two years&#146; performance sent the stock
sliding. This took place during a time when our company was challenged about social issues domestically and internationally. According to <I>The Wall Street Journal</I> (May 1, 2000) Boeing&#146;s $50 million advertising campaign was meant to
enhance its corporate image &#147;in large part . . . at its own 190,000 workers, many of them disillusioned after labor friction, production problems and waves of internal upheavals in recent years.&#148; The company refused to support shareholder
resolutions in 1998 and 1999 calling for adherence to basic human rights <I>at its own operations in China</I>. In its February 25, 2000 Annual Report on Human Rights, the U.S. State Department criticized China for a &#147;marked deterioration in
human rights.&#148; According to <I>The New York Times</I> (Feb. 1, 2000), Beijing has stepped up its intimidation against Catholic clergy loyal to the Pope and has destroyed church buildings.</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE BORDER="0" WIDTH="100%" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="2%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"></FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="Wingdings">&#159;</FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000"
FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="98%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Shareholders need to be vigilant and challenge executive pay packages that reward poor social or financial corporate
performance, asking themselves: if top officers&#146; pay for a given year should be reduced if the company suffers from poor corporate citizenship that harms our corporate image, such as costly fines, protracted labor tensions, poor image related
to lobbying on behalf of corrupt governments, or significant loss of market share to the likes of Airbus.</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE BORDER="0" WIDTH="100%" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="2%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"></FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="Wingdings">&#159;</FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000"
FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="98%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Surveys conducted and released by Boeing in 1999 show notable drops in employees&#146; confidence that the company is making
changes necessary to compete successfully, and that their jobs are secure (<I>WSJ</I> 05/01/00).</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE BORDER="0" WIDTH="100%" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="2%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"></FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="Wingdings">&#159;</FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000"
FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="98%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">IBM reported to shareholders how it integrates such factors into its compensation packages, understanding the need to balance
social criteria with fiscal concerns.</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="2">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"> RESOLVED: Shareholders request the Board to institute a special Executive Compensation Review to find ways to link compensation of its key executives not only with
fiscal performance but to social corporate performance as well. This shall include, but not be limited to, the company&#146;s efforts to promote basic human rights domestically and internationally within its operations. It shall also include a
comparison of the compensation packages for company officers with the lowest paid company employees in the U.S. and around the world. This review&#146;s results shall be made available to requesting shareholders by September 1, 2002.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Board of Directors&#146; Response</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The Compensation Committee of the Board, which consists of five non-employee, independent directors, oversees all the Company&#146;s executive compensation programs.
The Compensation Committee already provides a report to shareholders, beginning on page 29, which explains the criteria for CEO and executive officer compensation. As explained in that report, the goals of the Company&#146;s integrated executive
compensation programs are to attract, retain and motivate a high caliber executive leadership team, align executive compensation with shareholder interests, link pay to Company and individual performance, and achieve a balance between incentives for
short-term and long-term performance.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The Compensation Committee plays a key oversight role in ensuring that the Company&#146;s executives live up to the Company&#146;s commitment to being a good global
citizen. The Company recognizes that, in addition to delivering solid, sustained financial performance, it must be a socially responsible corporate citizen, both domestically and internationally. Good Corporate Citizenship is a value embedded in
Boeing&#146;s 2016 Vision Statement and has been integrated into many of the Company&#146;s assessment and development tools. In addition, specific performance criteria for the president of each operating group address how the group is performing
with respect to diversity. The Company is also committed to promoting the rule of law and showing respect for employees and their rights of association and assembly wherever we operate in the world. The Company is sensitive to issues of individual
rights and environmental degradation in other countries where it does business.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The Board agrees that executive compensation must be closely scrutinized&#151;precisely the function performed by independent directors of the Compensation Committee.
The Board believes, therefore, that the time, effort and expense necessary to complete the special review and produce the report requested are not justified.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

PROPOSAL 5</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>SHAREHOLDER PROPOSAL ON SIMPLE MAJORITY VOTES</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">A shareholder has advised the Company that he intends to present the following resolution at the Annual Meeting. In accordance with applicable proxy regulations, the
proposed resolution and supporting statement, for which the Board of Directors and the Company accept no responsibility, are set forth below. Approval of this proposal would require the affirmative vote of a majority of the outstanding shares of
Boeing stock present in person or by proxy and entitled to vote at the Annual Meeting.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Shareholder Resolution</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE WIDTH="100%" BORDER="0" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="4%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"></TD>
<TD ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">RESOLVED: Simple-Majority Vote Recommendation.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Boeing shareholders recommend the Board of Directors take steps
necessary to reinstate simple majority vote on all issues that are submitted for shareholder vote to the fullest extent possible. Recommend delete Boeing requirements for greater than a majority shareholder vote. Also, recommend that any future
action on this topic be put to shareholder vote&#151;as a separate proposal.</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Proponent&#146;s Supporting Statement</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Why return to simple majority vote?</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>

<TABLE BORDER="0" WIDTH="100%" CELLPADDING="0" CELLSPACING="0">
<TR>
<TD WIDTH="2%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"><DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"></FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="Wingdings">&#159;</FONT><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000"
FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
<TD WIDTH="98%" ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Simple-majority resolutions won approval averaging 54% from shareholders at major companies in 1999 and 2000&#151;<I>Investor
Responsibility Research Center</I>.</FONT></DIV>
</TD>
</TR>
</TABLE>

</TD>
</TR>

<TD ALIGN="left" VALIGN="top"> <DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Institutional Shareholder Services said that super-majority votes serve to lock in provisions that are harmful to shareholders. ISS said

<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">To increase shareholder value recommend:</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>S<SMALL>IMPLE</SMALL>
- -M<SMALL>AJORITY </SMALL>
V<SMALL>OTE</SMALL>
</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Y<SMALL>ES ON </SMALL>
5</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="3">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>Board of Directors&#146; Response</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">According to Delaware law, which governs the Company, most proposals submitted to a vote of the Company&#146;s shareholders, whether submitted by management or a
shareholder, require a vote of a majority of the shares present and eligible to vote at the meeting, whether in person or by proxy. This is apparently what the proponent is referring to as a &#147;simple-majority vote.&#148; In addition, Delaware
law imposes a greater voting requirement, of at least a majority of the outstanding shares entitled to vote, for certain fundamental corporate actions, such as amending the certificate of incorporation, approving certain mergers, selling
substantially all the assets or dissolving the corporation. The proposal <B>cannot</B> reduce this voting requirement to a &#147;simple majority vote.&#148; The proponent is mistaken in asserting that Boeing should &#147;return&#148; to a rule it
never had.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Delaware law also specifies that matters presented to shareholders may be subject to a greater percentage vote standard if that standard is set forth in a
company&#146;s certificate of incorporation. Consistent with Delaware law, the Company&#146;s charter documents, which were approved by shareholder vote, provide that the vote of holders of at least 75% of the Company&#146;s outstanding shares must
approve certain significant changes involving the Company. These changes include amendments to the by-laws or certificate of incorporation, significant mergers, sale of a significant proportion of assets and/or dissolution of the Company. For
example, significant transactions involving &#147;interested shareholders&#148; must be approved by 75% of the outstanding shares unless disinterested directors of the Company satisfy themselves as to the fairness of the transaction for <B>all
shareholders</B>. These &#147;super-majority&#148; provisions are designed to provide protection for all shareholders against the self-interest of a few large shareholders.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">The provisions are not intended to, and do not, preclude unsolicited offers to acquire the Company at a fair price. They are designed to encourage potential acquirers
to negotiate directly with the Board because the Company believes the Board is in the best position to evaluate the fairness of proposed offers, to negotiate on behalf of all shareholders and to protect shareholders from abusive tactics during a
takeover process. Adoption of a proposal for a &#147;simple majority vote on all issues&#148; would eliminate these protections.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"> The Company believes the proposal would also cause it to violate Delaware law. The proposal purports to require that matters be subject to a &#147;simple majority vote
on all issues&#148; without regard to the role of the Board of Directors. The Company could not, without violating Delaware law, permit adoption of any resolution by &#147;simple majority vote&#148; where such action is prohibited by law in the
absence of prior approval and recommendation by the Board of Directors.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">Approval of the proposal would not automatically eliminate the super-majority voting provisions described above, as this proposal is only a recommendation. If the
proposal were passed, the Board could only approve and recommend the proposed changes if it determined that they were in the best interests of the Company and its shareholders. If the Board were to approve the amendments, adoption would require the
favorable vote of shareholders owning 75% of the Company&#146;s outstanding shares.</FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>T<SMALL>HE </SMALL>
B<SMALL>OARD OF </SMALL>
D<SMALL>IRECTORS </SMALL>
U<SMALL>NANIMOUSLY </SMALL>
R<SMALL>ECOMMENDS</SMALL>
</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B><SMALL>A </SMALL>
V<SMALL>OTE </SMALL>
A<SMALL>GAINST </SMALL>
P<SMALL>ROPOSAL </SMALL>
5.</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"> <B>
<A NAME="753tx43"></A>
PROPOSAL 6</B></FONT></DIV>

<DIV ALIGN="center"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times"><B>ON ANNUAL ELECTION OF DIRECTORS</B></FONT></DIV>
<DIV><FONT SIZE="1">&nbsp;</FONT></DIV>
<DIV ALIGN="left"><FONT SIZE="3" COLOR="#000000" FACE="'Times New Roman', Times">A shareholder has advised the Company that it intends to present the following resolution at the Annual Meeting. In accordance with applicable proxy regulations, the
proposed resolution and supporting statement, for which the Board of Directors and the Company accept no responsibility, are set forth below. Approval of this proposal would require the affirmative vote of a majority of the outstanding shares of
Boeing stock present in person or by proxy and entitled to vote at the Annual Meeting.</FONT></DIV>

approval for this topic ranged from 47% to 49.9%. Shareholders request the Board of Directors take all necessary steps to adopt annual election of all directors as corporate policy.</FONT></DIV>

investors.</FONT></DIV>
